Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I came in because my mind just… it won’t hold onto things anymore. That’s the best way I can put it. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do without even thinking, honestly, almost in my sleep—and now the numbers and the words just scatter on me.

I’ll have the paper there, or the screen open, and I know what I’m trying to say at first, but then it’s like I can’t keep the pieces together long enough to get them down. I’ll look at a sentence and it doesn’t feel like it belongs to the thought I had a second before. It’s embarrassing, and frustrating, because it’s not some complicated thing I’m trying to do. It’s a simple note. And I used to handle that kind of thing easily. Now I just sit there staring at it, and everything slips away.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s… mostly when I’m trying to put things in order, I guess. Writing is where I notice it the most because it’s right there in front of me, you know? I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do almost without thinking—and I’ll start with the first line, and then the words just scatter. The numbers too, if there are any. I can’t seem to hold them in my head long enough to get them onto the page.

I don’t know that I can separate it neatly into “only writing” versus “everything else,” because it feels like the same problem underneath—my mind just won’t hold onto the pieces. But the writing makes it obvious. I’ll be looking at what I just wrote, and it’s like I’ve lost the thread of what I was trying to say. Then I sit there trying to reconstruct it, and the more I try, the more it feels like it slips away.

Now I just sit there staring at it, and everything slips away.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with holding onto things and keeping pieces together happen only when you're trying to write or work on a task, or does it happen in conversations or other parts of your daily life as well?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly when I’m trying to put things in order, I guess. Writing is where I notice it the most because it’s right there in front of me, you know? I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do almost without thinking—and I’ll start with the first line, and then the words just scatter. The numbers too, if there are any. I can’t seem to hold them in my head long enough to get them onto the page.\n\nI don’t know that I can separate it neatly into “only writing” versus “everything else,” because it feels like the same problem underneath—my mind just won’t hold onto the pieces. But the writing makes it obvious. I’ll be looking at what I just wrote, and it’s like I’ve lost the thread of what I was trying to say. Then I sit there trying to reconstruct it, and the more I try, the more it feels like it slips away.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"intent": "determine if memory issues are present in daily conversations", "question": "Do you find it hard to follow or remember what's being said when you're having a conversation with someone?"}

Yes… I think so, though it shows up most clearly when I’m trying to write something down. In conversation, I can be listening and then suddenly realize I’m holding only part of what the person said. Like I have the beginning of it, but the rest doesn’t stay put.

It’s the same feeling I was trying to describe—my mind just doesn’t hold onto things the way it used to. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I honestly could have done in my sleep before, and the numbers and words just scatter on me. And when someone’s talking, it can feel similar, like the words are coming in but I can’t keep them organized long enough to respond properly. I hate it, because I know I should be able to follow along. It’s not that I’m not trying. It’s like the thread just slips out of my hand.

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It feels like it’s there most of the time now, honestly. I mean, some moments are worse than others, but I don’t really get that old feeling back where my mind just holds the pieces together without effort.

Like with that simple note for the youth maker program—I know I keep coming back to that, but it’s because it should be so ordinary. I used to be able to do that almost automatically. Now I sit down, and I’ll have the first bit in my head, and then before I can get it down, the words scatter. If there are numbers involved, they don’t stay put either. It’s like I’m trying to carry water in my hands.

So I guess it comes and goes in intensity, but the problem itself feels pretty constant. Even when I’m not actively struggling with it, I’m sort of aware it could happen as soon as I try to organize something in my head.

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think that’s part of it. It’s not always like I forget a whole event, like it never happened, but I can’t hold onto the details of something that just happened, or what I was just about to do with it.

That’s honestly why I came in. My mind just will not hold onto things anymore. I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I could do in my sleep before, really, it was so routine—and I’ll have the words there for a second, and then they scatter. If there are numbers involved, same thing. I look at the page and I know I had it, I know it was just in my head, but it’s gone or broken into pieces before I can use it.

So yes, recent things… especially the kind I’m supposed to remember long enough to put in order or write down. It feels like they don’t stay with me the way they should.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, outside of writing too. It’s not just the notes or the numbers, though that’s where I notice it sharply. I’ve let a lot of things fall away.

I used to have responsibilities at church that I kept up with, and I was involved in the garden, and the youth maker program was something I cared about a great deal. That workshop especially used to feel like a place where I knew what I was doing. I could keep track of tools, steps, materials, what the kids were working on… and now I don’t feel safe being by myself in there. That’s hard to admit. It feels like saying I can’t be trusted with something that used to be part of who I was.

My wife has had to step in, and my pastor too, to cover things or help make decisions I should be handling. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t like leaving people to pick up what I’ve dropped. But I have dropped it. The church things, the garden, the maker program… I just haven’t been able to keep them going the way I used to.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n6\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.8, G31.84, R41.0, R41.1, R41.2, R41.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because my mind just… it won’t hold onto things anymore. That’s the best way I can put it. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do without even thinking, honestly, almost in my sleep—and now the numbers and the words just scatter on me.\n\nI’ll have the paper there, or the screen open, and I know what I’m trying to say at first, but then it’s like I can’t keep the pieces together long enough to get them down. I’ll look at a sentence and it doesn’t feel like it belongs to the thought I had a second before. It’s embarrassing, and frustrating, because it’s not some complicated thing I’m trying to do. It’s a simple note. And I used to handle that kind of thing easily. Now I just sit there staring at it, and everything slips away.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with holding onto things and keeping pieces together happen only when you're trying to write or work on a task, or does it happen in conversations or other parts of your daily life as well?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly when I’m trying to put things in order, I guess. Writing is where I notice it the most because it’s right there in front of me, you know? I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do almost without thinking—and I’ll start with the first line, and then the words just scatter. The numbers too, if there are any. I can’t seem to hold them in my head long enough to get them onto the page.\n\nI don’t know that I can separate it neatly into “only writing” versus “everything else,” because it feels like the same problem underneath—my mind just won’t hold onto the pieces. But the writing makes it obvious. I’ll be looking at what I just wrote, and it’s like I’ve lost the thread of what I was trying to say. Then I sit there trying to reconstruct it, and the more I try, the more it feels like it slips away.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to follow or remember what's being said when you're having a conversation with someone?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though it shows up most clearly when I’m trying to write something down. In conversation, I can be listening and then suddenly realize I’m holding only part of what the person said. Like I have the beginning of it, but the rest doesn’t stay put.\n\nIt’s the same feeling I was trying to describe—my mind just doesn’t hold onto things the way it used to. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I honestly could have done in my sleep before, and the numbers and words just scatter on me. And when someone’s talking, it can feel similar, like the words are coming in but I can’t keep them organized long enough to respond properly. I hate it, because I know I should be able to follow along. It’s not that I’m not trying. It’s like the thread just slips out of my hand.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with holding onto things and keeping pieces together all the time, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s there most of the time now, honestly. I mean, some moments are worse than others, but I don’t really get that old feeling back where my mind just holds the pieces together without effort.\n\nLike with that simple note for the youth maker program—I know I keep coming back to that, but it’s because it should be so ordinary. I used to be able to do that almost automatically. Now I sit down, and I’ll have the first bit in my head, and then before I can get it down, the words scatter. If there are numbers involved, they don’t stay put either. It’s like I’m trying to carry water in my hands.\n\nSo I guess it comes and goes in intensity, but the problem itself feels pretty constant. Even when I’m not actively struggling with it, I’m sort of aware it could happen as soon as I try to organize something in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of it. It’s not always like I forget a whole event, like it never happened, but I can’t hold onto the details of something that just happened, or what I was just about to do with it.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. My mind just will not hold onto things anymore. I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I could do in my sleep before, really, it was so routine—and I’ll have the words there for a second, and then they scatter. If there are numbers involved, same thing. I look at the page and I know I had it, I know it was just in my head, but it’s gone or broken into pieces before I can use it.\n\nSo yes, recent things… especially the kind I’m supposed to remember long enough to put in order or write down. It feels like they don’t stay with me the way they should.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble with daily tasks or routines outside of writing, like cooking, cleaning, or getting ready for the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, outside of writing too. It’s not just the notes or the numbers, though that’s where I notice it sharply. I’ve let a lot of things fall away.\n\nI used to have responsibilities at church that I kept up with, and I was involved in the garden, and the youth maker program was something I cared about a great deal. That workshop especially used to feel like a place where I knew what I was doing. I could keep track of tools, steps, materials, what the kids were working on… and now I don’t feel safe being by myself in there. That’s hard to admit. It feels like saying I can’t be trusted with something that used to be part of who I was.\n\nMy wife has had to step in, and my pastor too, to cover things or help make decisions I should be handling. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t like leaving people to pick up what I’ve dropped. But I have dropped it. The church things, the garden, the maker program… I just haven’t been able to keep them going the way I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Physically… the main thing I’ve noticed is food, I guess. It’s not exactly a strange sensation, but it feels like something has changed there.

Food just doesn’t have much appeal to me lately. Breakfast especially—I’ll sit there with it in front of me and sort of move it around the plate, take a bite or two because I know I’m supposed to, but there’s no pull toward it. Nothing sounds good in the way it used to. And then the day slips by, and by supper my wife will say something like, “You’ve hardly eaten today,” and I realize she’s right. I hadn’t really registered it.

It’s not that I’m trying not to eat. It’s more like the interest in it has gone quiet, if that makes sense. I can look at food and know it’s mealtime, but my body doesn’t seem to care much.

Now I just sit there staring at it, and everything slips away.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with holding onto things and keeping pieces together happen only when you're trying to write or work on a task, or does it happen in conversations or other parts of your daily life as well?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly when I’m trying to put things in order, I guess. Writing is where I notice it the most because it’s right there in front of me, you know? I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do almost without thinking—and I’ll start with the first line, and then the words just scatter. The numbers too, if there are any. I can’t seem to hold them in my head long enough to get them onto the page.\n\nI don’t know that I can separate it neatly into “only writing” versus “everything else,” because it feels like the same problem underneath—my mind just won’t hold onto the pieces. But the writing makes it obvious. I’ll be looking at what I just wrote, and it’s like I’ve lost the thread of what I was trying to say. Then I sit there trying to reconstruct it, and the more I try, the more it feels like it slips away.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to follow or remember what's being said when you're having a conversation with someone?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though it shows up most clearly when I’m trying to write something down. In conversation, I can be listening and then suddenly realize I’m holding only part of what the person said. Like I have the beginning of it, but the rest doesn’t stay put.\n\nIt’s the same feeling I was trying to describe—my mind just doesn’t hold onto things the way it used to. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I honestly could have done in my sleep before, and the numbers and words just scatter on me. And when someone’s talking, it can feel similar, like the words are coming in but I can’t keep them organized long enough to respond properly. I hate it, because I know I should be able to follow along. It’s not that I’m not trying. It’s like the thread just slips out of my hand.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with holding onto things and keeping pieces together all the time, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s there most of the time now, honestly. I mean, some moments are worse than others, but I don’t really get that old feeling back where my mind just holds the pieces together without effort.\n\nLike with that simple note for the youth maker program—I know I keep coming back to that, but it’s because it should be so ordinary. I used to be able to do that almost automatically. Now I sit down, and I’ll have the first bit in my head, and then before I can get it down, the words scatter. If there are numbers involved, they don’t stay put either. It’s like I’m trying to carry water in my hands.\n\nSo I guess it comes and goes in intensity, but the problem itself feels pretty constant. Even when I’m not actively struggling with it, I’m sort of aware it could happen as soon as I try to organize something in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of it. It’s not always like I forget a whole event, like it never happened, but I can’t hold onto the details of something that just happened, or what I was just about to do with it.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. My mind just will not hold onto things anymore. I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I could do in my sleep before, really, it was so routine—and I’ll have the words there for a second, and then they scatter. If there are numbers involved, same thing. I look at the page and I know I had it, I know it was just in my head, but it’s gone or broken into pieces before I can use it.\n\nSo yes, recent things… especially the kind I’m supposed to remember long enough to put in order or write down. It feels like they don’t stay with me the way they should.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble with daily tasks or routines outside of writing, like cooking, cleaning, or getting ready for the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, outside of writing too. It’s not just the notes or the numbers, though that’s where I notice it sharply. I’ve let a lot of things fall away.\n\nI used to have responsibilities at church that I kept up with, and I was involved in the garden, and the youth maker program was something I cared about a great deal. That workshop especially used to feel like a place where I knew what I was doing. I could keep track of tools, steps, materials, what the kids were working on… and now I don’t feel safe being by myself in there. That’s hard to admit. It feels like saying I can’t be trusted with something that used to be part of who I was.\n\nMy wife has had to step in, and my pastor too, to cover things or help make decisions I should be handling. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t like leaving people to pick up what I’ve dropped. But I have dropped it. The church things, the garden, the maker program… I just haven’t been able to keep them going the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health or experienced any unusual physical sensations lately?\n\nPatient: Physically… the main thing I’ve noticed is food, I guess. It’s not exactly a strange sensation, but it feels like something has changed there.\n\nFood just doesn’t have much appeal to me lately. Breakfast especially—I’ll sit there with it in front of me and sort of move it around the plate, take a bite or two because I know I’m supposed to, but there’s no pull toward it. Nothing sounds good in the way it used to. And then the day slips by, and by supper my wife will say something like, “You’ve hardly eaten today,” and I realize she’s right. I hadn’t really registered it.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying not to eat. It’s more like the interest in it has gone quiet, if that makes sense. I can look at food and know it’s mealtime, but my body doesn’t seem to care much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yes… most of the time, I think that’s fair to say. It’s not always crying sadness, if that makes sense. It’s more like I wake up and there’s already this heavy, hollow feeling sitting in my chest before the day has even started. Like I haven’t even had a chance to do anything wrong yet, and still I feel weighed down by it.

And hopeless… yes, that too, though I don’t like saying that out loud. My wife can be sitting right there, speaking gently to me, trying to encourage me, and I know she means it, I know she loves me, but it feels like I’m hearing her from very far away. Like I’m at the bottom of a well and she’s up above me, calling down, and I can understand the words but I can’t quite feel them reach me.

It’s there most days. That low, empty feeling. Even when nothing particular has happened. It just seems to be waiting for me when I wake up.

I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do without even thinking, honestly, almost in my sleep—and now the numbers and the words just scatter on me.\n\nI’ll have the paper there, or the screen open, and I know what I’m trying to say at first, but then it’s like I can’t keep the pieces together long enough to get them down. I’ll look at a sentence and it doesn’t feel like it belongs to the thought I had a second before. It’s embarrassing, and frustrating, because it’s not some complicated thing I’m trying to do. It’s a simple note. And I used to handle that kind of thing easily. Now I just sit there staring at it, and everything slips away.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with holding onto things and keeping pieces together happen only when you're trying to write or work on a task, or does it happen in conversations or other parts of your daily life as well?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly when I’m trying to put things in order, I guess. Writing is where I notice it the most because it’s right there in front of me, you know? I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do almost without thinking—and I’ll start with the first line, and then the words just scatter. The numbers too, if there are any. I can’t seem to hold them in my head long enough to get them onto the page.\n\nI don’t know that I can separate it neatly into “only writing” versus “everything else,” because it feels like the same problem underneath—my mind just won’t hold onto the pieces. But the writing makes it obvious. I’ll be looking at what I just wrote, and it’s like I’ve lost the thread of what I was trying to say. Then I sit there trying to reconstruct it, and the more I try, the more it feels like it slips away.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to follow or remember what's being said when you're having a conversation with someone?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though it shows up most clearly when I’m trying to write something down. In conversation, I can be listening and then suddenly realize I’m holding only part of what the person said. Like I have the beginning of it, but the rest doesn’t stay put.\n\nIt’s the same feeling I was trying to describe—my mind just doesn’t hold onto things the way it used to. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I honestly could have done in my sleep before, and the numbers and words just scatter on me. And when someone’s talking, it can feel similar, like the words are coming in but I can’t keep them organized long enough to respond properly. I hate it, because I know I should be able to follow along. It’s not that I’m not trying. It’s like the thread just slips out of my hand.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with holding onto things and keeping pieces together all the time, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s there most of the time now, honestly. I mean, some moments are worse than others, but I don’t really get that old feeling back where my mind just holds the pieces together without effort.\n\nLike with that simple note for the youth maker program—I know I keep coming back to that, but it’s because it should be so ordinary. I used to be able to do that almost automatically. Now I sit down, and I’ll have the first bit in my head, and then before I can get it down, the words scatter. If there are numbers involved, they don’t stay put either. It’s like I’m trying to carry water in my hands.\n\nSo I guess it comes and goes in intensity, but the problem itself feels pretty constant. Even when I’m not actively struggling with it, I’m sort of aware it could happen as soon as I try to organize something in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of it. It’s not always like I forget a whole event, like it never happened, but I can’t hold onto the details of something that just happened, or what I was just about to do with it.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. My mind just will not hold onto things anymore. I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I could do in my sleep before, really, it was so routine—and I’ll have the words there for a second, and then they scatter. If there are numbers involved, same thing. I look at the page and I know I had it, I know it was just in my head, but it’s gone or broken into pieces before I can use it.\n\nSo yes, recent things… especially the kind I’m supposed to remember long enough to put in order or write down. It feels like they don’t stay with me the way they should.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble with daily tasks or routines outside of writing, like cooking, cleaning, or getting ready for the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, outside of writing too. It’s not just the notes or the numbers, though that’s where I notice it sharply. I’ve let a lot of things fall away.\n\nI used to have responsibilities at church that I kept up with, and I was involved in the garden, and the youth maker program was something I cared about a great deal. That workshop especially used to feel like a place where I knew what I was doing. I could keep track of tools, steps, materials, what the kids were working on… and now I don’t feel safe being by myself in there. That’s hard to admit. It feels like saying I can’t be trusted with something that used to be part of who I was.\n\nMy wife has had to step in, and my pastor too, to cover things or help make decisions I should be handling. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t like leaving people to pick up what I’ve dropped. But I have dropped it. The church things, the garden, the maker program… I just haven’t been able to keep them going the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health or experienced any unusual physical sensations lately?\n\nPatient: Physically… the main thing I’ve noticed is food, I guess. It’s not exactly a strange sensation, but it feels like something has changed there.\n\nFood just doesn’t have much appeal to me lately. Breakfast especially—I’ll sit there with it in front of me and sort of move it around the plate, take a bite or two because I know I’m supposed to, but there’s no pull toward it. Nothing sounds good in the way it used to. And then the day slips by, and by supper my wife will say something like, “You’ve hardly eaten today,” and I realize she’s right. I hadn’t really registered it.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying not to eat. It’s more like the interest in it has gone quiet, if that makes sense. I can look at food and know it’s mealtime, but my body doesn’t seem to care much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or hopeless most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think that’s fair to say. It’s not always crying sadness, if that makes sense. It’s more like I wake up and there’s already this heavy, hollow feeling sitting in my chest before the day has even started. Like I haven’t even had a chance to do anything wrong yet, and still I feel weighed down by it.\n\nAnd hopeless… yes, that too, though I don’t like saying that out loud. My wife can be sitting right there, speaking gently to me, trying to encourage me, and I know she means it, I know she loves me, but it feels like I’m hearing her from very far away. Like I’m at the bottom of a well and she’s up above me, calling down, and I can understand the words but I can’t quite feel them reach me.\n\nIt’s there most days. That low, empty feeling. Even when nothing particular has happened. It just seems to be waiting for me when I wake up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F43.8", "G31.84", "R41.0", "R41.1", "R41.2", "R41.3"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I know sleep is something people ask about, and I’ve tried to pay attention, but I don’t know that I can give you a clear pattern.

Some nights feel different from others, but I can’t say I’ve noticed one specific change that I’m confident about. My wife might have a better sense of it than I do, honestly, because I don’t always trust my own read on what’s been happening. I’ll go to bed and then later I’m not sure if I slept poorly or if it just felt like a poor night because the day itself was hard.

So… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed anything definite enough to say, “Yes, this is what’s changed.” At least not in a way I can describe properly.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.8, G31.84, R41.0, R41.1, R41.2, R41.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because my mind just… it won’t hold onto things anymore. That’s the best way I can put it. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do without even thinking, honestly, almost in my sleep—and now the numbers and the words just scatter on me.\n\nI’ll have the paper there, or the screen open, and I know what I’m trying to say at first, but then it’s like I can’t keep the pieces together long enough to get them down. I’ll look at a sentence and it doesn’t feel like it belongs to the thought I had a second before. It’s embarrassing, and frustrating, because it’s not some complicated thing I’m trying to do. It’s a simple note. And I used to handle that kind of thing easily. Now I just sit there staring at it, and everything slips away.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with holding onto things and keeping pieces together happen only when you're trying to write or work on a task, or does it happen in conversations or other parts of your daily life as well?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly when I’m trying to put things in order, I guess. Writing is where I notice it the most because it’s right there in front of me, you know? I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do almost without thinking—and I’ll start with the first line, and then the words just scatter. The numbers too, if there are any. I can’t seem to hold them in my head long enough to get them onto the page.\n\nI don’t know that I can separate it neatly into “only writing” versus “everything else,” because it feels like the same problem underneath—my mind just won’t hold onto the pieces. But the writing makes it obvious. I’ll be looking at what I just wrote, and it’s like I’ve lost the thread of what I was trying to say. Then I sit there trying to reconstruct it, and the more I try, the more it feels like it slips away.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to follow or remember what's being said when you're having a conversation with someone?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though it shows up most clearly when I’m trying to write something down. In conversation, I can be listening and then suddenly realize I’m holding only part of what the person said. Like I have the beginning of it, but the rest doesn’t stay put.\n\nIt’s the same feeling I was trying to describe—my mind just doesn’t hold onto things the way it used to. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I honestly could have done in my sleep before, and the numbers and words just scatter on me. And when someone’s talking, it can feel similar, like the words are coming in but I can’t keep them organized long enough to respond properly. I hate it, because I know I should be able to follow along. It’s not that I’m not trying. It’s like the thread just slips out of my hand.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with holding onto things and keeping pieces together all the time, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s there most of the time now, honestly. I mean, some moments are worse than others, but I don’t really get that old feeling back where my mind just holds the pieces together without effort.\n\nLike with that simple note for the youth maker program—I know I keep coming back to that, but it’s because it should be so ordinary. I used to be able to do that almost automatically. Now I sit down, and I’ll have the first bit in my head, and then before I can get it down, the words scatter. If there are numbers involved, they don’t stay put either. It’s like I’m trying to carry water in my hands.\n\nSo I guess it comes and goes in intensity, but the problem itself feels pretty constant. Even when I’m not actively struggling with it, I’m sort of aware it could happen as soon as I try to organize something in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of it. It’s not always like I forget a whole event, like it never happened, but I can’t hold onto the details of something that just happened, or what I was just about to do with it.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. My mind just will not hold onto things anymore. I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I could do in my sleep before, really, it was so routine—and I’ll have the words there for a second, and then they scatter. If there are numbers involved, same thing. I look at the page and I know I had it, I know it was just in my head, but it’s gone or broken into pieces before I can use it.\n\nSo yes, recent things… especially the kind I’m supposed to remember long enough to put in order or write down. It feels like they don’t stay with me the way they should.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble with daily tasks or routines outside of writing, like cooking, cleaning, or getting ready for the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, outside of writing too. It’s not just the notes or the numbers, though that’s where I notice it sharply. I’ve let a lot of things fall away.\n\nI used to have responsibilities at church that I kept up with, and I was involved in the garden, and the youth maker program was something I cared about a great deal. That workshop especially used to feel like a place where I knew what I was doing. I could keep track of tools, steps, materials, what the kids were working on… and now I don’t feel safe being by myself in there. That’s hard to admit. It feels like saying I can’t be trusted with something that used to be part of who I was.\n\nMy wife has had to step in, and my pastor too, to cover things or help make decisions I should be handling. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t like leaving people to pick up what I’ve dropped. But I have dropped it. The church things, the garden, the maker program… I just haven’t been able to keep them going the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health or experienced any unusual physical sensations lately?\n\nPatient: Physically… the main thing I’ve noticed is food, I guess. It’s not exactly a strange sensation, but it feels like something has changed there.\n\nFood just doesn’t have much appeal to me lately. Breakfast especially—I’ll sit there with it in front of me and sort of move it around the plate, take a bite or two because I know I’m supposed to, but there’s no pull toward it. Nothing sounds good in the way it used to. And then the day slips by, and by supper my wife will say something like, “You’ve hardly eaten today,” and I realize she’s right. I hadn’t really registered it.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying not to eat. It’s more like the interest in it has gone quiet, if that makes sense. I can look at food and know it’s mealtime, but my body doesn’t seem to care much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or hopeless most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think that’s fair to say. It’s not always crying sadness, if that makes sense. It’s more like I wake up and there’s already this heavy, hollow feeling sitting in my chest before the day has even started. Like I haven’t even had a chance to do anything wrong yet, and still I feel weighed down by it.\n\nAnd hopeless… yes, that too, though I don’t like saying that out loud. My wife can be sitting right there, speaking gently to me, trying to encourage me, and I know she means it, I know she loves me, but it feels like I’m hearing her from very far away. Like I’m at the bottom of a well and she’s up above me, calling down, and I can understand the words but I can’t quite feel them reach me.\n\nIt’s there most days. That low, empty feeling. Even when nothing particular has happened. It just seems to be waiting for me when I wake up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I know sleep is something people ask about, and I’ve tried to pay attention, but I don’t know that I can give you a clear pattern.\n\nSome nights feel different from others, but I can’t say I’ve noticed one specific change that I’m confident about. My wife might have a better sense of it than I do, honestly, because I don’t always trust my own read on what’s been happening. I’ll go to bed and then later I’m not sure if I slept poorly or if it just felt like a poor night because the day itself was hard.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed anything definite enough to say, “Yes, this is what’s changed.” At least not in a way I can describe properly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

It just seems to be waiting for me when I wake up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I know sleep is something people ask about, and I’ve tried to pay attention, but I don’t know that I can give you a clear pattern.\n\nSome nights feel different from others, but I can’t say I’ve noticed one specific change that I’m confident about. My wife might have a better sense of it than I do, honestly, because I don’t always trust my own read on what’s been happening. I’ll go to bed and then later I’m not sure if I slept poorly or if it just felt like a poor night because the day itself was hard.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed anything definite enough to say, “Yes, this is what’s changed.” At least not in a way I can describe properly.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you found it harder to plan things out or make decisions about what needs to be done?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 67 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Diminished_Thinking\nmanifestation: \"I came in because my mind just will not hold onto things anymore—I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I could do in my sleep before, and the numbers and words scatter on me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Very much so. That’s actually a big part of why I came in, because my mind just… it doesn’t seem to hold onto things the way it used to.

I’ll sit down to do something simple, something I’ve done a hundred times before, like writing a note for the youth maker program, and I’ll have the paper in front of me and the pen in my hand, and then the whole thing just falls apart. I’ll know, in a general way, what I meant to say, but when I try to put it in order, the words scatter. Or there’ll be a couple of numbers I need to include, nothing complicated, and I’ll look at them and they won’t stay put in my head long enough for me to use them.

It’s embarrassing, honestly. This was the sort of thing I could do in my sleep before. Make a list, plan the next step, write a short note, decide what needs to happen first and what can wait. Now I can sit there for far too long and still not have anything useful written down. It’s like I keep reaching for the thread of the thought, and it slips away before I can tie it to the next one.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.8, G31.84, R41.0, R41.1, R41.2, R41.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because my mind just… it won’t hold onto things anymore. That’s the best way I can put it. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do without even thinking, honestly, almost in my sleep—and now the numbers and the words just scatter on me.\n\nI’ll have the paper there, or the screen open, and I know what I’m trying to say at first, but then it’s like I can’t keep the pieces together long enough to get them down. I’ll look at a sentence and it doesn’t feel like it belongs to the thought I had a second before. It’s embarrassing, and frustrating, because it’s not some complicated thing I’m trying to do. It’s a simple note. And I used to handle that kind of thing easily. Now I just sit there staring at it, and everything slips away.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with holding onto things and keeping pieces together happen only when you're trying to write or work on a task, or does it happen in conversations or other parts of your daily life as well?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly when I’m trying to put things in order, I guess. Writing is where I notice it the most because it’s right there in front of me, you know? I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do almost without thinking—and I’ll start with the first line, and then the words just scatter. The numbers too, if there are any. I can’t seem to hold them in my head long enough to get them onto the page.\n\nI don’t know that I can separate it neatly into “only writing” versus “everything else,” because it feels like the same problem underneath—my mind just won’t hold onto the pieces. But the writing makes it obvious. I’ll be looking at what I just wrote, and it’s like I’ve lost the thread of what I was trying to say. Then I sit there trying to reconstruct it, and the more I try, the more it feels like it slips away.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to follow or remember what's being said when you're having a conversation with someone?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though it shows up most clearly when I’m trying to write something down. In conversation, I can be listening and then suddenly realize I’m holding only part of what the person said. Like I have the beginning of it, but the rest doesn’t stay put.\n\nIt’s the same feeling I was trying to describe—my mind just doesn’t hold onto things the way it used to. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I honestly could have done in my sleep before, and the numbers and words just scatter on me. And when someone’s talking, it can feel similar, like the words are coming in but I can’t keep them organized long enough to respond properly. I hate it, because I know I should be able to follow along. It’s not that I’m not trying. It’s like the thread just slips out of my hand.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with holding onto things and keeping pieces together all the time, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s there most of the time now, honestly. I mean, some moments are worse than others, but I don’t really get that old feeling back where my mind just holds the pieces together without effort.\n\nLike with that simple note for the youth maker program—I know I keep coming back to that, but it’s because it should be so ordinary. I used to be able to do that almost automatically. Now I sit down, and I’ll have the first bit in my head, and then before I can get it down, the words scatter. If there are numbers involved, they don’t stay put either. It’s like I’m trying to carry water in my hands.\n\nSo I guess it comes and goes in intensity, but the problem itself feels pretty constant. Even when I’m not actively struggling with it, I’m sort of aware it could happen as soon as I try to organize something in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of it. It’s not always like I forget a whole event, like it never happened, but I can’t hold onto the details of something that just happened, or what I was just about to do with it.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. My mind just will not hold onto things anymore. I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I could do in my sleep before, really, it was so routine—and I’ll have the words there for a second, and then they scatter. If there are numbers involved, same thing. I look at the page and I know I had it, I know it was just in my head, but it’s gone or broken into pieces before I can use it.\n\nSo yes, recent things… especially the kind I’m supposed to remember long enough to put in order or write down. It feels like they don’t stay with me the way they should.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble with daily tasks or routines outside of writing, like cooking, cleaning, or getting ready for the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, outside of writing too. It’s not just the notes or the numbers, though that’s where I notice it sharply. I’ve let a lot of things fall away.\n\nI used to have responsibilities at church that I kept up with, and I was involved in the garden, and the youth maker program was something I cared about a great deal. That workshop especially used to feel like a place where I knew what I was doing. I could keep track of tools, steps, materials, what the kids were working on… and now I don’t feel safe being by myself in there. That’s hard to admit. It feels like saying I can’t be trusted with something that used to be part of who I was.\n\nMy wife has had to step in, and my pastor too, to cover things or help make decisions I should be handling. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t like leaving people to pick up what I’ve dropped. But I have dropped it. The church things, the garden, the maker program… I just haven’t been able to keep them going the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health or experienced any unusual physical sensations lately?\n\nPatient: Physically… the main thing I’ve noticed is food, I guess. It’s not exactly a strange sensation, but it feels like something has changed there.\n\nFood just doesn’t have much appeal to me lately. Breakfast especially—I’ll sit there with it in front of me and sort of move it around the plate, take a bite or two because I know I’m supposed to, but there’s no pull toward it. Nothing sounds good in the way it used to. And then the day slips by, and by supper my wife will say something like, “You’ve hardly eaten today,” and I realize she’s right. I hadn’t really registered it.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying not to eat. It’s more like the interest in it has gone quiet, if that makes sense. I can look at food and know it’s mealtime, but my body doesn’t seem to care much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or hopeless most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think that’s fair to say. It’s not always crying sadness, if that makes sense. It’s more like I wake up and there’s already this heavy, hollow feeling sitting in my chest before the day has even started. Like I haven’t even had a chance to do anything wrong yet, and still I feel weighed down by it.\n\nAnd hopeless… yes, that too, though I don’t like saying that out loud. My wife can be sitting right there, speaking gently to me, trying to encourage me, and I know she means it, I know she loves me, but it feels like I’m hearing her from very far away. Like I’m at the bottom of a well and she’s up above me, calling down, and I can understand the words but I can’t quite feel them reach me.\n\nIt’s there most days. That low, empty feeling. Even when nothing particular has happened. It just seems to be waiting for me when I wake up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I’ll go to bed and then later I’m not sure if I slept poorly or if it just felt like a poor night because the day itself was hard.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Like I haven’t even had a chance to do anything wrong yet, and still I feel weighed down by it.\n\nAnd hopeless… yes, that too, though I don’t like saying that out loud. My wife can be sitting right there, speaking gently to me, trying to encourage me, and I know she means it, I know she loves me, but it feels like I’m hearing her from very far away. Like I’m at the bottom of a well and she’s up above me, calling down, and I can understand the words but I can’t quite feel them reach me.\n\nIt’s there most days. That low, empty feeling. Even when nothing particular has happened. It just seems to be waiting for me when I wake up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I know sleep is something people ask about, and I’ve tried to pay attention, but I don’t know that I can give you a clear pattern.\n\nSome nights feel different from others, but I can’t say I’ve noticed one specific change that I’m confident about. My wife might have a better sense of it than I do, honestly, because I don’t always trust my own read on what’s been happening. I’ll go to bed and then later I’m not sure if I slept poorly or if it just felt like a poor night because the day itself was hard.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed anything definite enough to say, “Yes, this is what’s changed.” At least not in a way I can describe properly.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to plan things out or make decisions about what needs to be done?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. That’s actually a big part of why I came in, because my mind just… it doesn’t seem to hold onto things the way it used to.\n\nI’ll sit down to do something simple, something I’ve done a hundred times before, like writing a note for the youth maker program, and I’ll have the paper in front of me and the pen in my hand, and then the whole thing just falls apart. I’ll know, in a general way, what I meant to say, but when I try to put it in order, the words scatter. Or there’ll be a couple of numbers I need to include, nothing complicated, and I’ll look at them and they won’t stay put in my head long enough for me to use them.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. This was the sort of thing I could do in my sleep before. Make a list, plan the next step, write a short note, decide what needs to happen first and what can wait. Now I can sit there for far too long and still not have anything useful written down. It’s like I keep reaching for the thread of the thought, and it slips away before I can tie it to the next one.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have trouble recalling familiar words or names when speaking?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I do. Not every single sentence, but often enough that it scares me a little.

It’s like the word is there somewhere, I can feel the shape of it, but I can’t get hold of it. And names, too — familiar names, people I know, or things I’ve said a thousand times. I’ll be talking and then suddenly there’s this blank space where the word should be, and I just sort of circle around it. I’ll say, “You know, the thing,” or I’ll describe it awkwardly because the actual word won’t come.

That’s part of what I meant before about my mind not holding onto things anymore. I came in because it feels like my thoughts just won’t stay in place. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program — something I used to be able to do practically in my sleep — and the words scatter on me. Even little numbers I need to include, they won’t stay put long enough. It makes me feel foolish, and honestly, frightened, because it’s familiar things, not complicated things.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.8, G31.84\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because my mind just… it won’t hold onto things anymore. That’s the best way I can put it. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do without even thinking, honestly, almost in my sleep—and now the numbers and the words just scatter on me.\n\nI’ll have the paper there, or the screen open, and I know what I’m trying to say at first, but then it’s like I can’t keep the pieces together long enough to get them down. I’ll look at a sentence and it doesn’t feel like it belongs to the thought I had a second before. It’s embarrassing, and frustrating, because it’s not some complicated thing I’m trying to do. It’s a simple note. And I used to handle that kind of thing easily. Now I just sit there staring at it, and everything slips away.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with holding onto things and keeping pieces together happen only when you're trying to write or work on a task, or does it happen in conversations or other parts of your daily life as well?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly when I’m trying to put things in order, I guess. Writing is where I notice it the most because it’s right there in front of me, you know? I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do almost without thinking—and I’ll start with the first line, and then the words just scatter. The numbers too, if there are any. I can’t seem to hold them in my head long enough to get them onto the page.\n\nI don’t know that I can separate it neatly into “only writing” versus “everything else,” because it feels like the same problem underneath—my mind just won’t hold onto the pieces. But the writing makes it obvious. I’ll be looking at what I just wrote, and it’s like I’ve lost the thread of what I was trying to say. Then I sit there trying to reconstruct it, and the more I try, the more it feels like it slips away.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to follow or remember what's being said when you're having a conversation with someone?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though it shows up most clearly when I’m trying to write something down. In conversation, I can be listening and then suddenly realize I’m holding only part of what the person said. Like I have the beginning of it, but the rest doesn’t stay put.\n\nIt’s the same feeling I was trying to describe—my mind just doesn’t hold onto things the way it used to. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I honestly could have done in my sleep before, and the numbers and words just scatter on me. And when someone’s talking, it can feel similar, like the words are coming in but I can’t keep them organized long enough to respond properly. I hate it, because I know I should be able to follow along. It’s not that I’m not trying. It’s like the thread just slips out of my hand.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with holding onto things and keeping pieces together all the time, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s there most of the time now, honestly. I mean, some moments are worse than others, but I don’t really get that old feeling back where my mind just holds the pieces together without effort.\n\nLike with that simple note for the youth maker program—I know I keep coming back to that, but it’s because it should be so ordinary. I used to be able to do that almost automatically. Now I sit down, and I’ll have the first bit in my head, and then before I can get it down, the words scatter. If there are numbers involved, they don’t stay put either. It’s like I’m trying to carry water in my hands.\n\nSo I guess it comes and goes in intensity, but the problem itself feels pretty constant. Even when I’m not actively struggling with it, I’m sort of aware it could happen as soon as I try to organize something in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of it. It’s not always like I forget a whole event, like it never happened, but I can’t hold onto the details of something that just happened, or what I was just about to do with it.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. My mind just will not hold onto things anymore. I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I could do in my sleep before, really, it was so routine—and I’ll have the words there for a second, and then they scatter. If there are numbers involved, same thing. I look at the page and I know I had it, I know it was just in my head, but it’s gone or broken into pieces before I can use it.\n\nSo yes, recent things… especially the kind I’m supposed to remember long enough to put in order or write down. It feels like they don’t stay with me the way they should.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble with daily tasks or routines outside of writing, like cooking, cleaning, or getting ready for the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, outside of writing too. It’s not just the notes or the numbers, though that’s where I notice it sharply. I’ve let a lot of things fall away.\n\nI used to have responsibilities at church that I kept up with, and I was involved in the garden, and the youth maker program was something I cared about a great deal. That workshop especially used to feel like a place where I knew what I was doing. I could keep track of tools, steps, materials, what the kids were working on… and now I don’t feel safe being by myself in there. That’s hard to admit. It feels like saying I can’t be trusted with something that used to be part of who I was.\n\nMy wife has had to step in, and my pastor too, to cover things or help make decisions I should be handling. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t like leaving people to pick up what I’ve dropped. But I have dropped it. The church things, the garden, the maker program… I just haven’t been able to keep them going the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health or experienced any unusual physical sensations lately?\n\nPatient: Physically… the main thing I’ve noticed is food, I guess. It’s not exactly a strange sensation, but it feels like something has changed there.\n\nFood just doesn’t have much appeal to me lately. Breakfast especially—I’ll sit there with it in front of me and sort of move it around the plate, take a bite or two because I know I’m supposed to, but there’s no pull toward it. Nothing sounds good in the way it used to. And then the day slips by, and by supper my wife will say something like, “You’ve hardly eaten today,” and I realize she’s right. I hadn’t really registered it.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying not to eat. It’s more like the interest in it has gone quiet, if that makes sense. I can look at food and know it’s mealtime, but my body doesn’t seem to care much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or hopeless most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think that’s fair to say. It’s not always crying sadness, if that makes sense. It’s more like I wake up and there’s already this heavy, hollow feeling sitting in my chest before the day has even started. Like I haven’t even had a chance to do anything wrong yet, and still I feel weighed down by it.\n\nAnd hopeless… yes, that too, though I don’t like saying that out loud. My wife can be sitting right there, speaking gently to me, trying to encourage me, and I know she means it, I know she loves me, but it feels like I’m hearing her from very far away. Like I’m at the bottom of a well and she’s up above me, calling down, and I can understand the words but I can’t quite feel them reach me.\n\nIt’s there most days. That low, empty feeling. Even when nothing particular has happened. It just seems to be waiting for me when I wake up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I know sleep is something people ask about, and I’ve tried to pay attention, but I don’t know that I can give you a clear pattern.\n\nSome nights feel different from others, but I can’t say I’ve noticed one specific change that I’m confident about. My wife might have a better sense of it than I do, honestly, because I don’t always trust my own read on what’s been happening. I’ll go to bed and then later I’m not sure if I slept poorly or if it just felt like a poor night because the day itself was hard.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed anything definite enough to say, “Yes, this is what’s changed.” At least not in a way I can describe properly.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to plan things out or make decisions about what needs to be done?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. That’s actually a big part of why I came in, because my mind just… it doesn’t seem to hold onto things the way it used to.\n\nI’ll sit down to do something simple, something I’ve done a hundred times before, like writing a note for the youth maker program, and I’ll have the paper in front of me and the pen in my hand, and then the whole thing just falls apart. I’ll know, in a general way, what I meant to say, but when I try to put it in order, the words scatter. Or there’ll be a couple of numbers I need to include, nothing complicated, and I’ll look at them and they won’t stay put in my head long enough for me to use them.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. It’s like I keep reaching for the thread of the thought, and it slips away before I can tie it to the next one.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble recalling familiar words or names when speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. Nothing sounds good in the way it used to. And then the day slips by, and by supper my wife will say something like, “You’ve hardly eaten today,” and I realize she’s right. I hadn’t really registered it.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying not to eat. It’s more like the interest in it has gone quiet, if that makes sense. I can look at food and know it’s mealtime, but my body doesn’t seem to care much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or hopeless most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think that’s fair to say. It’s not always crying sadness, if that makes sense. It’s more like I wake up and there’s already this heavy, hollow feeling sitting in my chest before the day has even started. Like I haven’t even had a chance to do anything wrong yet, and still I feel weighed down by it.\n\nAnd hopeless… yes, that too, though I don’t like saying that out loud. My wife can be sitting right there, speaking gently to me, trying to encourage me, and I know she means it, I know she loves me, but it feels like I’m hearing her from very far away. Like I’m at the bottom of a well and she’s up above me, calling down, and I can understand the words but I can’t quite feel them reach me.\n\nIt’s there most days. That low, empty feeling. Even when nothing particular has happened. It just seems to be waiting for me when I wake up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I know sleep is something people ask about, and I’ve tried to pay attention, but I don’t know that I can give you a clear pattern.\n\nSome nights feel different from others, but I can’t say I’ve noticed one specific change that I’m confident about. My wife might have a better sense of it than I do, honestly, because I don’t always trust my own read on what’s been happening. I’ll go to bed and then later I’m not sure if I slept poorly or if it just felt like a poor night because the day itself was hard.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed anything definite enough to say, “Yes, this is what’s changed.” At least not in a way I can describe properly.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to plan things out or make decisions about what needs to be done?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. That’s actually a big part of why I came in, because my mind just… it doesn’t seem to hold onto things the way it used to.\n\nI’ll sit down to do something simple, something I’ve done a hundred times before, like writing a note for the youth maker program, and I’ll have the paper in front of me and the pen in my hand, and then the whole thing just falls apart. I’ll know, in a general way, what I meant to say, but when I try to put it in order, the words scatter. Or there’ll be a couple of numbers I need to include, nothing complicated, and I’ll look at them and they won’t stay put in my head long enough for me to use them.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. This was the sort of thing I could do in my sleep before. Make a list, plan the next step, write a short note, decide what needs to happen first and what can wait. Now I can sit there for far too long and still not have anything useful written down. It’s like I keep reaching for the thread of the thought, and it slips away before I can tie it to the next one.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble recalling familiar words or names when speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. Not every single sentence, but often enough that it scares me a little.\n\nIt’s like the word is there somewhere, I can feel the shape of it, but I can’t get hold of it. And names, too — familiar names, people I know, or things I’ve said a thousand times. I’ll be talking and then suddenly there’s this blank space where the word should be, and I just sort of circle around it. I’ll say, “You know, the thing,” or I’ll describe it awkwardly because the actual word won’t come.\n\nThat’s part of what I meant before about my mind not holding onto things anymore. I came in because it feels like my thoughts just won’t stay in place. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program — something I used to be able to do practically in my sleep — and the words scatter on me. I don’t know that I’d say that, no. Not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.

I mean, if you asked me to recall something from years ago in perfect detail, I’m sure I’d be like anyone else my age — some things are sharp, some things are blurry, and some things I’d need a little prompting for. But important events, important conversations… I haven’t really noticed those disappearing on me. At least, not in some obvious way where I think, “How could I not remember that?”

It’s more that I sometimes get uncertain when I’m trying to talk about things, and then I second-guess myself. I’ll wonder, “Did I tell that right?” or “Am I mixing up the order?” But I don’t know if that’s memory exactly, or just me not feeling very steady when I’m trying to explain something.

So… no, I can’t point to important things from my past that I’ve lost. If anything, some of those older things are still there more clearly than whatever I’m trying to say in the moment.

I’ll say, “You know, the thing,” or I’ll describe it awkwardly because the actual word won’t come.\n\nThat’s part of what I meant before about my mind not holding onto things anymore. I came in because it feels like my thoughts just won’t stay in place. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program — something I used to be able to do practically in my sleep — and the words scatter on me. Even little numbers I need to include, they won’t stay put long enough. It makes me feel foolish, and honestly, frightened, because it’s familiar things, not complicated things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble recalling important events or conversations from your past?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say that, no. Not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, if you asked me to recall something from years ago in perfect detail, I’m sure I’d be like anyone else my age — some things are sharp, some things are blurry, and some things I’d need a little prompting for. But important events, important conversations… I haven’t really noticed those disappearing on me. At least, not in some obvious way where I think, “How could I not remember that?”\n\nIt’s more that I sometimes get uncertain when I’m trying to talk about things, and then I second-guess myself. I’ll wonder, “Did I tell that right?” or “Am I mixing up the order?” But I don’t know if that’s memory exactly, or just me not feeling very steady when I’m trying to explain something.\n\nSo… no, I can’t point to important things from my past that I’ve lost. It feels new in the sense that I don’t recognize myself in it. I mean, maybe it crept up a little and I didn’t want to admit it at first, but the way it is now… it feels like something has changed.

I came in because my mind just will not hold onto things anymore. That’s the simplest way I can say it. I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program — something I could do in my sleep before, honestly, without even thinking much about it — and suddenly I can’t keep the pieces together. The words scatter, the numbers scatter, and I’m left staring at the page like I’ve never done this sort of thing before.

So I don’t know if it was gradual in the background, but it’s become very noticeable to me now. It’s not just ordinary forgetfulness, at least it doesn’t feel that way. It feels like I’m trying to hold water in my hands.
